[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 1.7 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"05ec67","wall_time_seconds":1.5429637,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3873,"output":"# Facts, Rules, and What Follows\\r\\n\\r\\nTreat the given statements as true. Your job is to find what else must be true.\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore you combine statements, identify what each one says. A **fact** describes a case. A **rule** tells you what must follow if its condition is met.\\r\\n\\r\\nA **conclusion** is what the statements prove. It can follow from a fact and a rule, or from a valid chain of rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n## 01 | Facts state what is true\\r\\n\\r\\nA fact tells you what is true in a particular case. **“Application J was submitted after the deadline”** tells us something about Application J. It does not tell us when other applications were submitted.\\r\\n\\r\\nA fact can also describe more than one case. **“Some applications were submitted after the deadline”** establishes that at least one late application exists. It does not establish that every application was late.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor now, keep those claims separate from anything you might infer. “J was late” is given. Whether J requires special approval is a different claim. We need a rule connecting lateness to that requirement before we can prove it.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- FACT-SCOPE -->\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep the whole description. “Requires special approval” is different from “has received special approval.” Each could be a fact about an application, but a requirement does not establish that it has been fulfilled.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n## 02 | Rules state a requirement\\r\\n\\r\\nA rule tells you what must follow if its condition is met. Consider this policy: **Every application submitted after the deadline requires special approval.**\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule has two complete parts. **Submitted after the deadline** is the condition. **Requires special approval** is the required result. The rule connects them in that order.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- RULE-PARTS -->\\r\\n\\r\\nLet **L** mean “submitted after the deadline” and **R** mean “requires special approval.” Then we can write the same rule as **L → R**. The arrow points from the condition to the required result. If L is true, R must be true.\\r\\n\\r\\nBeing late is **sufficient** to establish the approval requirement. That means lateness is enough to trigger this rule. Requiring approval is **necessary** for a late application. That means a late application cannot lack that requirement.\\r\\n\\r\\nThose words describe this connection. They do not say that approval is the only requirement, or that lateness is the only reason approval could be required.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule alone does not establish that any application was late. It tells us what is required of any late application, even if no applications were submitted late.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Keep the kind of claim intact\\r\\n\\r\\nA rule can tell us what someone **should** do or what they **may** do. That is different from telling us what they actually did.\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose **every damaged item should be inspected**. If this item is damaged, it should be inspected. We cannot conclude it has been inspected. If it is not true that an item should be inspected, it cannot be damaged under this rule. But “has not been inspected” is not the same as “it is not true that it should be inspected.” An inspection could be overdue.\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose **a visitor may enter the archive only if the visitor has a badge**. A badge is necessary for permission to enter. The rule is **May enter → Has a badge**. Without a badge, the visitor does not have permission to enter under this rule. Having a badge alone does not establish permission; another requirement might still apply.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- MODALITY -->\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule concerns permission. It does not prove that someone physically cannot enter without permission. Keep the same kind of claim throughout your reasoning: a requirement stays a requirement, advice stays advice, and permission stays permission.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every damaged item should be inspected. This damaged item has not been inspected. Does that violate the rule? What can you conclude about what should happen?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** The item should be inspected. Its not having been inspected does not contradict the claim about what should happen. The rule does not state that every required inspection actually occurs.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 03 | Apply one rule to a fact\\r\\n\\r\\nNow combine a rule with a fact that meets its condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** Every application submitted after the deadline requires special approval.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Fact:** Application J was submitted after the deadline.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Conclusion:** Application J requires special approval.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe fact supplies the condition. The rule supplies the connection to the result. Together, they prove the conclusion.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- FACT-LAB -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every file marked confidential requires restricted storage. This file is marked confidential. What follows? Does the rule alone establish that a confidential file exists?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** This file requires restricted storage. The fact establishes that this particular file is confidential. The rule alone establishes a requirement for any confidential file, without establishing that any such file exists.\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are two ways to use the application policy. A late application must require approval. An application that does **not** require approval cannot have been late. If it had been late, the policy would require approval, contradicting the fact we were given.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- TWO-MOVES -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Forward:** Submitted late → Requires approval.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Contrapositive:** Does not require approval → Was not submitted late.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe contrapositive states the same rule from its other end. Reverse the direction **and negate both complete ideas**. Simply reversing the arrow would say that requiring approval proves lateness. The original policy never said that.\\r\\n\\r\\nSimply negating both ideas without reversing would also add something: “not late → does not require approval.” An on-time application could require approval for a different reason. That situation does not break the policy.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe only combination the original rule rules out is **late, but does not require approval**. The other three combinations can fit the rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- CASE-GRID -->\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- POP-QUIZ-1 -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every confidential file requires restricted storage. This file does not require restricted storage. What follows? What if all you know is that it does require restricted storage?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** If it does not require restricted storage, it is not confidential. That is the contrapositive. If it does require restricted storage, its confidentiality remains unknown. A nonconfidential file could require restricted storage for another reason.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 04 | Negate the whole requirement\\r\\n\\r\\nSome conditions contain more than one part. Suppose **a document may be released only if it has been reviewed AND authorized**. Permission requires both parts. If either part is missing, the combined requirement is not satisfied.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** May be released → Reviewed AND authorized.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Contrapositive:** Not reviewed OR not authorized → May not be released.\\r\\n\\r\\nTo negate “reviewed AND authorized,” you only need at least one failure. You do not need both parts to fail. “Not both” does not mean “neither.”\\r\\n\\r\\nNow suppose **a visitor may enter only if the visitor has a badge OR an invitation**. This requirement allows either route, including both. To fail the entire requirement, the visitor must have neither.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** May enter → Has badge OR has invitation.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Contrapositive:** No badge AND no invitation → May not enter.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- NEGATION -->\\r\\n\\r\\nNegating a compound condition changes **AND to OR**, or **OR to AND**, while negating each part. For the original AND requirement, one missing part is enough. For the original OR requirement, both routes must be missing.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** A document may be released only if it has been reviewed and authorized. It has been reviewed but not authorized. What follows? Does the contrapositive require us to conclude it was not reviewed?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** The document may not be released. Missing authorization is enough to fail the combined requirement. We do not conclude that review is missing too; the fact says review occurred.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 05 | Connect rules at a matching middle\\r\\n\\r\\nAdd a second policy: **Every application that requires special approval needs a supervisor\'s signature.** The first rule gets us to “requires special approval.” The second starts at that exact idea. We can carry the application through both rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n**First rule:** Submitted late → Requires special approval.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Second rule:** Requires special approval → Needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Combined rule:** Submitted late → Needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn letters, the pattern is L → R and R → S, so L → S. If a particular application was late, both requirements follow for that application. Without a fact establishing a late application, we still have a combined rule rather than proof that a late application exists.\\r\\n\\r\\nYou can also work backward through the combined rule. If an application does **not** need a supervisor\'s signature, it cannot require special approval under the second policy. If it does not require special approval, it cannot have been late under the first policy.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Backward chain:** Does not need signature → Does not require approval → Was not late.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- CHAIN-LAB -->\\r\\n\\r\\n### Matching a word is not enough\\r\\n\\r\\n“Requires special approval” and “has received special approval” both mention approval. They describe different conditions. A requirement can exist before anyone fulfills it. You cannot link a rule ending in the first phrase to a rule starting in the second unless another statement connects them.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is why you should diagram **complete ideas**, not isolated words. Keep words such as “requires,” “has,” “should,” and “may” inside the condition they qualify.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every late application requires special approval. Every application that has received special approval has a supervisor\'s signature. Does a late application necessarily have a supervisor\'s signature? Explain the missing connection.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** No. A late application requires approval, but it may not have received it. The second rule applies to applications that have received approval. Nothing says the late application\'s requirement has been fulfilled.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 06 | Shared beginnings do not create a chain\\r\\n\\r\\nCompare a different pair of rules: **Every late application requires special approval. Every late application needs a supervisor\'s signature.** Both rules start at “late.” Neither starts where the other ends.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- SHARED-TRIGGER -->\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- POP-QUIZ-2 -->\\r\\n\\r\\nIf a fact tells us an application was late, we can apply **both** rules to it. That application requires approval and needs a signature. But we cannot conclude that every application requiring approval needs a signature. An on-time application might require approval for another reason without needing a signature.\\r\\n\\r\\nLikewise, two rules that end at the same idea do not connect their starting conditions. If confidential files require restricted storage and medical files require restricted storage, a confidential file need not be a medical file. Restricted storage could cover both types separately.\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore joining two arrows, find the destination of the first and the starting condition of the second. They must match, including any negation or qualifier. If they do not match, keep the rules separate or see whether a valid contrapositive produces a match.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every member of a review panel has training. Every member of that panel has a security badge. Must everyone with training have a security badge? What follows if Dana is a panel member?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** Training alone does not guarantee a badge. A trained person could be outside the panel. Because Dana is a panel member, both rules apply to Dana: Dana has training and a security badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 07 | Choose the move and test the claim\\r\\n\\r\\nStart by separating **facts about a case** from **rules about cases**. Then choose the move the information supports.\\r\\n\\r\\n| What you have | The move | What to check |\\r\\n| --- | --- | --- |\\r\\n| A fact meeting a rule\'s starting condition | Apply the rule forward | Does the whole fact match the whole condition? |\\r\\n| A fact denying a rule\'s required result | Use the contrapositive | Did you reverse and negate both complete ideas? |\\r\\n| Two rules with a matching middle condition | Link the rules | Are direction, negation, and qualifiers identical? |\\r\\n| Only the required result, or only the absence of the starting condition | Leave the other side open | Could another explanation fit the rule? |\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen an inference seems tempting, try to build a case in which the statements are true and the proposed conclusion is false. If you can, the conclusion is not guaranteed. The counterexamples above work this way: an on-time application can still require approval; a requirement can remain unfulfilled; a trained person can be outside the panel.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep the scope of the fact too. **Some applications were submitted late. Every late application requires special approval. Therefore, some applications require special approval.** We cannot change “some” to “all.” The rule covers every late case, but the fact establishes only at least one. Other applications could be on time and require no special approval.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 08 | Mix the moves\\r\\n\\r\\nFor each problem, write what follows and name the move. If a proposed conclusion is not guaranteed, explain a situation that would make it false while keeping the given statements true.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 1:** Every confidential file requires restricted storage. File K does not require restricted storage. What follows?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** File K is not confidential. Use the contrapositive: does not require restricted storage → not confidential.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 2:** Every late application requires special approval. Every application requiring special approval needs a supervisor\'s signature. Application J was late. What follows?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** Application J requires special approval and needs a supervisor\'s signature. Apply the first rule to the fact, then carry its result through the second rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 3:** Every panel member has training. Every panel member has a security badge. Lee has training. Must Lee have a badge?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** No. Lee could be a trained person outside the panel who has no badge. The two rules share a starting condition. Training alone does not establish that Lee meets it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 4:** A document may be released only if it has been reviewed and authorized. Document M has not been reviewed. What follows? Must it also lack authorization?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** Document M may not be released. One missing requirement is enough. Whether it has authorization remains open; it could have authorization but lack review.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 09 | Take this into your assigned work\\r\\n\\r\\nDo the **conditional-diagramming drills first**, then move to the **LSAT questions**. In the drills, write the complete conditions before shortening them. Check your arrow and its contrapositive. When two rules appear, look for an exact match before combining them.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn the LSAT questions, separate the facts from the rules before choosing an answer. Your job is to identify what the statements guarantee. If an answer reverses the rule, drops a qualifier, or turns a requirement into an accomplished action, test it with a counterexample.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep this question nearby: **What do I actually have, and which move does it support?**\\r\\n"}'}]

It does not establish that every application was late.\\r\\nbrainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap/concept-lesson-v5.md:67:<!-- FACT-LAB -->\\r\\nbrainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap/concept-lesson-v5.md:81:The contrapositive states the same rule from its other end. Reverse the direction **and negate both complete ideas**. Simply reversing the arrow would say that requiring approval proves lateness. The original policy never said that.\\r\\nbrainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap/concept-lesson-v5.md:93:**Worked answer:** If it does not require restricted storage, it is not confidential. That is the contrapositive. If it does require restricted storage, its confidentiality remains unknown. A nonconfidential file could require restricted storage for another reason.\\r\\nbrainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap/concept-lesson-v5.md:97:Some conditions contain more than one part. Suppose **a document may be released only if it has been reviewed AND authorized**. Permission requires both parts. If either part is missing, the combined requirement is not satisfied.\\r\\nbrainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap/concept-lesson-v5.md:115:**Try it:** A document may be released only if it has been reviewed and authorized. It has been reviewed but not authorized. What follows? Does the contrapositive require us to conclude it was not reviewed?\\r\\nbrainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap/concept-lesson-v5.md:159:Before joining two arrows, find the destination of the first and the starting condition of the second. They must match, including any negation or qualifier. If they do not match, keep the rules separate or see whether a valid contrapositive produces a match.\\r\\nbrainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap/concept-lesson-v5.md:172:| A fact denying a rule\'s required result | Use the contrapositive | Did you reverse and negate both complete ideas? |\\r\\nbrainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap/concept-lesson-v5.md:178:Keep the scope of the fact too. **Some applications were submitted late. Every late application requires special approval. Therefore, some applications require special approval.** We cannot change “some” to “all.” The rule covers every late case, but the fact establishes only at least one. Other applications could be on time and require no special approval.\\r\\nbrainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap/concept-lesson-v5.md:186:**Worked answer:** File K is not confidential. Use the contrapositive: does not require restricted storage → not confidential.\\r\\nbrainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap/concept-lesson-v5.md:202:Do the **conditional-diagramming drills first**, then move to the **LSAT questions**. In the drills, write the complete conditions before shortening them. Check your arrow and its contrapositive. When two rules appear, look for an exact match before combining them.\\r\\nbrainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap/build-preview-v5.py:62:base.VISUALS[\'FACT-LAB\'] = base.FACT_LAB\\nbrainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap/build-preview-v5.py:64:base.VISUALS[\'FACT-SCOPE\'] = \'\'\'<figure class=\\"fact-scope\\"><div><span>One identified case</span><strong>Application J<br>was late.</strong><p>J\'s submission time is given. Other applications are not described.</p></div><div><span>At least one case</span><strong>Some applications<br>were late.</strong><p>A late application exists. The statement does not say all were late.</p></div><figcaption>A fact\'s scope tells you how much is established before you infer anything approval</strong></div><div class=\\"signal-track second\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\"></div><div class=\\"flow-node result-node\\"><span>Guaranteed result</span><strong id=\\"proof-result\\">Requires special approval</strong></div></div><div class=\\"fact-display\\" aria-live=\\"polite\\"><span id=\\"fact-move\\">Apply forward</span><p id=\\"fact-result\\">It requires special approval.</p><p id=\\"fact-reason\\">The fact meets the rule\'s starting condition. The requirement follows.</p></div><p class=\\"lab-foot\\">Change the given fact. The policy stays the same.</p></section>\'\'\'\\r\\nbase.VISUALS[\'TWO-MOVES\'] = \'\'\'<figure class=\\"two-moves\\"><div class=\\"move forward\\"><span class=\\"move-name\\">Forward</span><div class=\\"movement\\"><span class=\\"given\\">Submitted late</span><span class=\\"moving-arrow\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\">→</span><span class=\\"follows\\">Requires approval</span></div><p>You have the starting condition. The required result follows.</p></div><div class=\\"move backward\\"><span class=\\"move-name\\">Contrapositive</span><div class=\\"movement\\"><span class=\\"given\\">Does not require approval</span><span class=\\"moving-arrow\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\">→</span><span class=\\"follows\\">Not submitted late</span></div><p>The required result is absent. The starting condition is ruled out.</p></div><figcaption>Reverse the direction. Negate both complete ideas. Preserve the rule.</figcaption></figure>\'\'\'\\r\\nbase.VISUALS[\'SHARED-TRIGGER\'] = \'\'\'<figure class=\\"fork\\"><div class=\\"fork-root\\"><small>The shared trigger</small><strong>Submitted late</strong></div><svg viewBox=\\"0 0 600 90\\" role=\\"img\\" aria-label=\\"Two separate arrows lead from submitted late to two requirements\\"><path d=\\"M300 3V28Q300 45 275 45H120V78M300 28Q300 45 325 45H480V78\\" fill=\\"none\\" stroke=\\"currentColor\\" stroke-width=\\"3\\"/><path d=\\"M113 68L120 80L127 68M473 68L480 80L487 68\\" fill=\\"none\\" stroke=\\"currentColor\\" stroke-width=\\"3\\"/></svg><div class=\\"fork-results\\"><span>Requires approval</span><span>Needs a signature</span></div><div class=\\"missing-link\\"><span aria-hidden=\\"true\\">↔</span> No connection between these results is given.</div><figcaption>A late application has both requirements. An application needing approval could be on time and not need a signature.</figcaption></figure>\'\'\'\\r\\nbase.VISUALS[\'POP-QUIZ-1\'] = quiz(1, \'The application requires approval. Was it late?\', [\'It must have been late.\', \'It could have been late. We cannot tell.\', \'It could not have been late.\'], 1, \'The middle answer is correct. An on-time application could require approval for another reason. Both a late application requiring approval and an on-time application requiring approval fit the rule.\')\\r\\nbase.VISUALS[\'POP-QUIZ-2\'] = quiz(2, \'Late applications need approval and a signature. What does approval alone prove?\', [\'The application was late.\', \'The application needs a signature.\', \'Neither conclusion is guaranteed.\'], 2, \'Neither is guaranteed. An on-time application could require approval without needing a signature. Both original rules still hold because neither is triggered by that application.\')\\r\\n\\r\\nbase.FACT_LAB = base.FACT_LAB.replace(\'<label for=\\"fact-choice\\">Give the application one fact</label>\', \'<fieldset class=\\"fact-picks\\"><legend>Give the application one fact</legend><button type=\\"button\\" data-fact=\\"late\\" aria-pressed=\\"true\\">Submitted late</button><button type=\\"button\\" data-fact=\\"notapproval\\" aria-pressed=\\"false\\">Approval not required</button><button type=\\"button\\" data-fact=\\"approval\\" aria-pressed=\\"false\\">Approval required</button><button type=\\"button\\" data-fact=\\"notlate\\" aria-pressed=\\"false\\">Not submitted late</button></fieldset><details class=\\"alternate-control\\"><summary>Choose from a list</summary><label for=\\"fact-choice\\">Given fact</label>\').replace(\'</select><div class=\\"proof-flow\\"\', \'</select></details><div class=\\"proof-flow\\"\')\\r\\nbase.VISUALS[\'FACT-LAB\'] = base.FACT_LAB\\r\\nbase.FACT_LAB = \'\'\'<figure class=\\"inference-overview\\"><div><span>Given information</span><strong>Read each whole idea.</strong></div><span class=\\"overview-arrow\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\">↓</span><div><span>Supported move</span><strong>Find what the statements guarantee.</strong></div><span class=\\"overview-arrow\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\">↓</span><div><span>Conclusion</span><strong>Keep only what must follow.</strong></div><figcaption>Inference means proving a new claim from the information you are given.</figcaption></figure>\'\'\'\\r\\nbase.VISUALS[\'FACT-SCOPE\'] = \'\'\'<figure class=\\"fact-scope\\"><div><span>One identified case</span><strong>Application J<br>was late.</strong><p>J\'s submission time is given. Other applications are not described.</p></div><div><span>At least one case</span><strong>Some applications<br>were late.</strong><p>A late application exists. The statement does not say all were late.</p></div><figcaption>A fact\'s scope tells you how much is established before you infer anything else.</figcaption></figure>\'\'\'\\r\\nbase.VISUALS[\'RULE-PARTS\'] = \'\'\'<figure class=\\"rule-parts\\"><div><span>Condition</span><strong>Submitted after<br>the deadline</strong></div><span class=\\"thick-arrow\\" aria-label=\\"requires\\">→</span><div><span>Required result</span><strong>Requires<br>special approval</strong></div><figcaption>If the condition is met, the result is required. The arrow does not work backward by itself.</figcaption></figure>\'\'\'\\r\\n\\r\\nCSS = [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 1.8 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"e2b03d","wall_time_seconds":1.5710601,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1865,"output":"# Facts, Rules, and What Follows\\r\\n\\r\\nConditional reasoning uses rules to work out what must follow from the information given.\\r\\n\\r\\nTreat the given statements as true. Your job is to find what else must be true.\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore you combine statements, identify what each one says. A **fact** describes a case. A **rule** tells you what must follow if its condition is met.\\r\\n\\r\\nA **conclusion** is what the statements prove. It can follow from a fact and a rule, or from a valid chain of rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n## 01 | Facts state what is true\\r\\n\\r\\nA fact tells you what is true in a particular case. **“Application J was submitted after the deadline”** tells us something about Application J. It does not tell us when other applications were submitted.\\r\\n\\r\\nA fact can also describe more than one case. **“Some applications were submitted after the deadline”** establishes that at least one late application exists. It does not establish that every application was late.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor now, keep those claims separate from anything you might infer. “J was late” is given. Whether J requires special approval is a different claim. We need a rule connecting lateness to that requirement before we can prove it.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- FACT-SCOPE -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Some reports are missing pages. Does at least one report with missing pages exist? Must every report be missing pages?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** At least one report is missing pages. The statement establishes that such a report exists, but does not establish that every report is missing pages. Other reports could be complete.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep the whole description. “Requires special approval” is different from “has received special approval.” Each could be a fact about an application, but a requirement does not establish that it has been fulfilled.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n## 02 | Rules state a requirement\\r\\n\\r\\nA rule tells you what must follow if its condition is met. Consider this policy: **Every application submitted after the deadline requires special approval.**\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule has two complete parts. **Submitted after the deadline** is the condition. **Requires special approval** is the required result. The rule connects them in that order.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- RULE-PARTS -->\\r\\n\\r\\nLet **L** mean “submitted after the deadline” and **R** mean “requires special approval.” Then we can write the same rule as **L → R**. The arrow points from the condition to the required result. If L is true, R must be true.\\r\\n\\r\\nBeing late is **sufficient** to establish the approval requirement. That means lateness is enough to trigger this rule. Requiring approval is **necessary** for a late application. That means a late application cannot lack that requirement.\\r\\n\\r\\nThose words describe this connection. They do not say that approval is the only requirement, or that lateness is the only reason approval could be required.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule alone does not establish that any application was late. It tells us what is required of any late application, even if no applications were submitted late.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Keep the kind of claim intact\\r\\n\\r\\nA rule can tell us what someone **should** do or what they **may** do. That is different from telling us what they actually did.\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose **every damaged item should be inspected**. If this item is damaged, it should be inspected. We cannot conclude it has been inspected. But “has not been inspected” is not the same as “it is not true that it should be inspected.” An inspection could be overdue.\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose **a visitor may enter the archive only if the visitor has a badge**. A badge is necessary for permission to enter. The rule is **May enter → Has a badge**. Without a badge, the visitor does not have permission to enter under this rule. Having a badge alone does not establish permission; another requirement might still apply.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- MODALITY -->\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule concerns permission. It does not prove that someone physically cannot enter without permission. Keep the same kind of claim throughout your reasoning: a requirement stays a requirement, advice stays advice, and permission stays permission.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every damaged item should be inspected. This damaged item has not been inspected. Does that violate the rule? What can you conclude about what should happen?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** The item should be inspected. Its not having been inspected does not contradict the claim about what should happen. The rule does not state that every required inspection actually occurs.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 03 | Apply one rule to a fact\\r\\n\\r\\nNow combine a rule with a fact that meets its condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** Every application submitted after the deadline requires special approval.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Fact:** Application J was submitted after the deadline.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Conclusion:** Application J requires special approval.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe fact supplies the condition. The rule supplies the connection to the result. Together, they prove the conclusion.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every file marked confidential requires restricted storage. This file is marked confidential. What follows? Does the rule alone establish that a confidential file exists?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** This file requires restricted storage. The fact establishes that this particular file is confidential. The rule alone establishes a requirement for any confidential file, without establishing that any such file exists.\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are two ways to use the application policy. A late application must require approval. An application that does **not** require approval cannot have been late. If it had been late, the policy would require approval, contradicting the fact we were given.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- TWO-MOVES -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Forward:** Submitted late → Requires approval.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Contrapositive:** Does not require approval → Was not submitted late.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe contrapositive states the same rule from its other end. Reverse the direction **and negate both complete ideas**. Simply reversing the arrow would say that requiring approval proves lateness. The original policy never said that.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep any qualifier inside the condition when you negate it. For “damaged → should be inspected,” the contrapositive begins with “it is not true that it should be inspected,” and concludes “not damaged.” Whether the item has actually been inspected is a separate claim.\\r\\n\\r\\nSimply negating both ideas without reversing would also add something: “not late → does not require approval.” An on-time application could require approval for a different reason. That situation does not break the policy.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe only combination the original rule rules out is **late, but does not require approval**. The other three combinations can fit the rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- FACT-LAB -->\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- CASE-GRID -->\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- POP-QUIZ-1 -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every confidential file requires restricted storage. This file does not require restricted storage. What follows? What if all you know is that it does require restricted storage?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** If it does not require restricted storage, it is not confidential. That is the contrapositive. If it does require restricted storage, its confidentiality remains unknown. A nonconfidential file could require restricted storage for another reason.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 04 | Negate the whole requirement\\r\\n\\r\\nSome conditions contain more than one part. Suppose **a document may be released only if it has been reviewed AND authorized**. Permission requires both parts. If either part is missing, the combined requirement is not satisfied.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** May be released → Reviewed AND authorized.\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 1.8 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"98f962","wall_time_seconds":1.5957501,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":4971,"output":"# Facts, Rules, and What Follows\\r\\n\\r\\nConditional reasoning uses general rules to work out what must follow from the information given.\\r\\n\\r\\nFirst, understand each statement. Then decide whether you are **applying a rule to a case** or **linking rules to make a new rule**. Those moves produce different kinds of conclusions.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn an inference question, accept all the given statements as true. Your job is to prove what else must be true, without adding a fact or a connection the statements never supplied.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n## 01 | Facts establish information about a case\\r\\n\\r\\n**Definition:** A fact is information established about a particular case or group. A fact given in the question is treated as true. A fact you infer must be proved from the given information.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Concrete example:** “Mira is trained.” That tells us something about Mira. It does not tell us whether other people are trained, whether Mira has a badge, or how training relates to badges.\\r\\n\\r\\nA fact can be negative: **“Niko does not have a badge.”** It can describe a group: **“Some staff members are trained.”** That last statement establishes at least one trained staff member, without establishing that every staff member is trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- FACT-SCOPE -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Some reports are missing pages. Does at least one report with missing pages exist? Must every report be missing pages?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** At least one report is missing pages. The statement establishes that such a report exists, but does not establish that every report is missing pages. Other reports could be complete.\\r\\n\\r\\nBoth facts and rules are accepted as true when they are given. Sort them by their role: a case fact establishes that a condition is true of someone or something; a conditional rule connects that condition to another.\\r\\n\\r\\n“Requires approval” and “has received approval” are different facts. A requirement can be established without establishing that it was fulfilled. Keep the complete claim when you read or shorten it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 02 | Rules connect conditions\\r\\n\\r\\n**Definition:** A conditional rule is a general relationship: every case meeting its starting condition must also meet its result condition. A rule connects conditions; it does not identify a particular case that meets them.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Concrete example:** “Everyone who is trained has a badge.” This is a relationship between being trained and having a badge. It is not merely a policy about what people ought to do. It says that every trained person actually has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- RULE-PARTS -->\\r\\n\\r\\nLet **T** mean “is trained” and **B** mean “has a badge.” We can write the rule as **T → B**. The arrow points from the starting condition to the required result. If T is true of someone, B must be true of that same person.\\r\\n\\r\\nBeing trained is **sufficient** to establish that someone has a badge: it is enough to trigger this rule. Having a badge is **necessary** for being trained under this rule: a trained person cannot lack a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\nThat does not mean training is the only way to get a badge. It also does not establish that anyone is trained. The rule could hold even if no one met its starting condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Rules can connect different kinds of claims\\r\\n\\r\\nA rule can tell us what someone **should** do or what they **may** do. That is different from telling us what they actually did.\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose **every damaged item should be inspected**. If this item is damaged, it should be inspected. We cannot conclude it has been inspected. But “has not been inspected” is not the same as “it is not true that it should be inspected.” An inspection could be overdue.\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose **a visitor may enter the archive only if the visitor has a badge**. A badge is necessary for permission to enter. The rule is **May enter → Has a badge**. Without a badge, the visitor does not have permission to enter under this rule. Having a badge alone does not establish permission; another requirement might still apply.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- MODALITY -->\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule concerns permission. It does not prove that someone physically cannot enter without permission. Keep the same kind of claim throughout your reasoning: a requirement stays a requirement, advice stays advice, and permission stays permission.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every damaged item should be inspected. This damaged item has not been inspected. Does that violate the rule? What can you conclude about what should happen?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** The item should be inspected. Its not having been inspected does not contradict the claim about what should happen. The rule does not state that every required inspection actually occurs.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 03 | Apply a rule to the same case\\r\\n\\r\\n**Definition:** Applying a rule means finding an established fact that satisfies the rule\'s starting condition, then concluding the required result for **the same case**.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Apply the rule forward\\r\\n\\r\\n**Given fact:** Mira is trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** Everyone who is trained has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** “Mira is trained” satisfies the rule\'s condition, “is trained.”\\r\\n\\r\\n**Therefore:** Mira has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** The rule covers every trained person, including Mira. We have proved a new fact about Mira. We have not made a claim about everyone who has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- APPLY-SEQUENCE -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every file marked confidential requires restricted storage. This file is marked confidential. What follows? Does the rule alone establish that a confidential file exists?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** This file requires restricted storage. The fact establishes that this particular file is confidential. The rule alone establishes a requirement for any confidential file, without establishing that any such file exists.\\r\\n\\r\\n### When the fact denies the result\\r\\n\\r\\nA different fact calls for a different move. Suppose **Niko does not have a badge**, and the same rule says **everyone who is trained has a badge**.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf Niko were trained, the rule would require Niko to have a badge. That would contradict the given fact. So **Niko is not trained**.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Given fact:** Niko does not have a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** Trained → Has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** The fact denies the required result. Restate the rule as **No badge → Not trained**.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Therefore:** Niko is not trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** Training cannot be present while the rule\'s required result is absent. This is the **contrapositive**: reverse the arrow and negate both complete conditions.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe contrapositive states the same rule. It does not deny the original rule, and it is not the unsupported reversal **Has a badge → Trained**.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep qualifiers inside the conditions. For **Damaged → Should be inspected**, the contrapositive starts with “it is not true that it should be inspected” and concludes “not damaged.” Whether the item has actually been inspected is a different claim.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- TWO-MOVES -->\\r\\n\\r\\n### Four facts, one unchanged rule\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule is **Trained → Has a badge**. Read what each fact actually matches before choosing a conclusion.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fact 1: The person is trained\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** The original starting condition is met.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** The person has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** Every trained person has a badge under this rule. Apply it to this person.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fact 2: The person has no badge\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** The required result is absent. This meets the contrapositive\'s starting condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** The person is not trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** A trained person without a badge would contradict the rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fact 3: The person has a badge\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** We have only the original result. The rule does not say that everyone with a badge is trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** Whether this person is trained remains unknown.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** An untrained visitor with a badge fits the rule. A trained person with a badge also fits it.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fact 4: The person is not trained\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** The original starting condition is absent. The rule only guarantees badges for trained people.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** Whether this person has a badge remains unknown.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** An untrained person could have a badge or lack one. Both situations fit the rule.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe forbidden combination is **trained, but no badge**. All other combinations can fit the rule. A rule rules out that combination; a fact tells us which condition we actually have.\\r\\n\\r\\nNow explore those same moves with a different rule: **Every late application requires special approval.** First identify the supplied fact. Then see whether it triggers the original rule, triggers its contrapositive, or leaves the other condition open.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- FACT-LAB -->\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- CASE-GRID -->\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- POP-QUIZ-1 -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every confidential file requires restricted storage. This file does not require restricted storage. What follows? What if all you know is that it does require restricted storage?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** If it does not require restricted storage, it is not confidential. That is the contrapositive. If it does require restricted storage, its confidentiality remains unknown. A nonconfidential file could require restricted storage for another reason.\\r\\n\\r\\nYou do not have to write every contrapositive automatically. If the fact clearly meets the original starting condition, apply that rule. If the fact denies its result, or a connection is difficult to see, a contrapositive can make the supported move clear.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 04 | Negate the whole requirement\\r\\n\\r\\nSome conditions contain more than one part. Suppose **a document may be released only if it has been reviewed AND authorized**. Permission requires both parts. If either part is missing, the combined requirement is not satisfied.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** May be released → Reviewed AND authorized.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Contrapositive:** Not reviewed OR not authorized → May not be released.\\r\\n\\r\\nTo negate “reviewed AND authorized,” you only need at least one failure. You do not need both parts to fail. “Not both” does not mean “neither.”\\r\\n\\r\\nNow suppose **a visitor may enter only if the visitor has a badge OR an invitation**. This requirement allows either route, including both. To fail the entire requirement, the visitor must have neither.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** May enter → Has badge OR has invitation.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Contrapositive:** No badge AND no invitation → May not enter.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- NEGATION -->\\r\\n\\r\\nNegating a compound condition changes **AND to OR**, or **OR to AND**, while negating each part. For the original AND requirement, one missing part is enough. For the original OR requirement, both routes must be missing.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** A document may be released only if it has been reviewed and authorized. It has been reviewed but not authorized. What follows? Does the contrapositive require us to conclude it was not reviewed?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** The document may not be released. Missing authorization is enough to fail the combined requirement. We do not conclude that review is missing too; the fact says review occurred.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 05 | Link rules to produce a new rule\\r\\n\\r\\n**Definition:** Linking rules means matching the result of one rule to the starting condition of another, so that together they establish a **new conditional rule**.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Link the rules without adding a case\\r\\n\\r\\n**First rule:** Every late application requires special approval.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Second rule:** Every application requiring special approval needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** “Requires special approval” is the result of the first rule and the starting condition of the second. The whole idea matches.\\r\\n\\r\\n**New rule:** Submitted late → Requires special approval → Needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Therefore:** Every late application needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** Consider any application that is late. The first rule requires approval; that satisfies the second rule\'s condition, which requires a signature. This proves the general connection **Late → Needs signature**. It does not establish that any application was late.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Add a fact and use the whole chain\\r\\n\\r\\n**Given fact:** Application J was submitted late.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rules:** Submitted late → Requires special approval → Needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Therefore:** Application J requires special approval and needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rules-only conclusion was a new **if-then rule**. Adding J\'s fact lets us apply it and conclude something about **J**. Do not silently add a case when the information gives you only rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- TWO-OUTPUTS -->\\r\\n\\r\\nYou can also use the chain\'s contrapositive: **Does not need signature → Does not require approval → Was not late**. A fact denying the final result can be carried backward through both connections.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- CHAIN-LAB -->\\r\\n\\r\\n### The whole middle idea must match\\r\\n\\r\\n“Requires special approval” and “has received special approval” are not the same condition. A requirement can remain unfulfilled. A rule ending in the first phrase cannot be linked to one starting in the second unless another statement connects them. Shared words are not enough; match the complete claim, including qualifiers and negation.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every late application requires special approval. Every application that has received special approval has a supervisor\'s signature. Does a late application necessarily have a supervisor\'s signature? Explain the missing connection.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** No. A late application requires approval, but it may not have received it. The second rule applies to applications that have received approval. Nothing says the late application\'s requirement has been fulfilled.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A contrapositive can uncover the match\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose these two rules are given:\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule 1:** Trained → Has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule 2:** Not trained → Requires supervision.\\r\\n\\r\\n“Trained” and “not trained” are opposite ideas in the same position: both are starting conditions. They do not look like a chain yet. Take the contrapositive of the first rule:\\r\\n\\r\\n**Restated rule 1:** No badge → Not trained.\\r\\n\\r\\nNow “not trained” is the result of that restatement and the starting condition of rule 2. We can link them:\\r\\n\\r\\n**New chain:** No badge → Not trained → Requires supervision.\\r\\n\\r\\n**New conditional:** Anyone without a badge requires supervision.\\r\\n\\r\\nAgain, that is a general rule. It does not prove that someone lacks a badge. If a separate fact says Niko lacks a badge, then we can apply the chain and conclude that Niko requires supervision.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- HIDDEN-CHAIN -->\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen opposite ideas sit in the same position, try a contrapositive to expose a match. When the match is already visible, use it directly. The point is to find a supported connection, not to produce extra diagrams for their own sake.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 06 | Shared beginnings do not create a chain\\r\\n\\r\\nCompare a different pair of rules: **Every late application requires special approval. Every late application needs a supervisor\'s signature.** Both rules start at “late.” Neither starts where the other ends.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- SHARED-TRIGGER -->\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- POP-QUIZ-2 -->\\r\\n\\r\\nIf a fact tells us an application was late, we can apply **both** rules to it. That application requires approval and needs a signature. But we cannot conclude that every application requiring approval needs a signature. An on-time application might require approval for another reason without needing a signature.\\r\\n\\r\\nLikewise, two rules that end at the same idea do not connect their starting conditions. If confidential files require restricted storage and medical files require restricted storage, a confidential file need not be a medical file. Restricted storage could cover both types separately.\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore joining two arrows, find the destination of the first and the starting condition of the second. They must match, including any negation or qualifier. If they do not match, keep the rules separate or see whether a valid contrapositive produces a match.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every member of a review panel has training. Every member of that panel has a security badge. Must everyone with training have a security badge? What follows if Dana is a panel member?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** Training alone does not guarantee a badge. A trained person could be outside the panel. Because Dana is a panel member, both rules apply to Dana: Dana has training and a security badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 07 | Choose the move and test the claim\\r\\n\\r\\nStart by separating **facts about a case** from **rules about cases**. Then choose the move the information supports.\\r\\n\\r\\n| What you have | The move | What to check |\\r\\n| --- | --- | --- |\\r\\n| A fact meeting a rule\'s starting condition | Apply the rule forward | Does the whole fact match the whole condition? |\\r\\n| A fact denying a rule\'s required result | Use the contrapositive | Did you reverse and negate both complete ideas? |\\r\\n| Two rules with a matching middle condition | Link the rules | Are direction, negation, and qualifiers identical? |\\r\\n| Only the required result, or only the absence of the starting condition | Leave the other side open | Could another explanation fit the rule? |\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen an inference seems tempting, try to build a case in which the statements are true and the proposed conclusion is false. If you can, the conclusion is not guaranteed. The counterexamples above work this way: an on-time application can still require approval; a requirement can remain unfulfilled; a trained person can be outside the panel.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep the scope of the fact too. **Some applications were submitted late. Every late application requires special approval. Therefore, some applications require special approval.** We cannot change “some” to “all.” The rule covers every late case, but the fact establishes only at least one. Other applications could be on time and require no special approval.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 08 | Mix the moves\\r\\n\\r\\nFor each problem, write what follows and name the move. If a proposed conclusion is not guaranteed, explain a situation that would make it false while keeping the given statements true.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 1:** Every confidential file requires restricted storage. File K does not require restricted storage. What follows?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** File K is not confidential. Use the contrapositive: does not require restricted storage → not confidential.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 2:** Every late application requires special approval. Every application requiring special approval needs a supervisor\'s signature. Application J was late. What follows?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** Application J requires special approval and needs a supervisor\'s signature. Apply the first rule to the fact, then carry its result through the second rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 3:** Every panel member has training. Every panel member has a security badge. Lee has training. Must Lee have a badge?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** No. Lee could be a trained person outside the panel who has no badge. The two rules share a starting condition. Training alone does not establish that Lee meets it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 4:** A document may be released only if it has been reviewed and authorized. Document M has not been reviewed. What follows? Must it also lack authorization?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** Document M may not be released. One missing requirement is enough. Whether it has authorization remains open; it could have authorization but lack review.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 09 | Take this into your assigned work\\r\\n\\r\\nDo the **conditional-diagramming drills first**, then move to the **LSAT questions**. In the drills, write the complete conditions before shortening them. Check your arrow and its contrapositive. When two rules appear, look for an exact match before combining them.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn the LSAT questions, separate the facts from the rules before choosing an answer. Your job is to identify what the statements guarantee. If an answer reverses the rule, drops a qualifier, or turns a requirement into an accomplished action, test it with a counterexample.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep this question nearby: **What do I actually have, and which move does it support?**\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 6.8 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"58391a","wall_time_seconds":6.5727152,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":4989,"output":"# Facts, Rules, and What Follows\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery trained person has a badge. Start with that rule. Here are two different ways to use it.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1 | Apply a rule to a fact\\r\\n\\r\\n**Fact:** Mira is trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** Every trained person has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Conclusion:** Mira has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** Mira meets the rule\'s starting condition: she is trained. The rule tells us she has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Result:** A conclusion about Mira.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2 | Link two rules\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule 1:** Every trained person has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule 2:** Every badge holder has building access.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Conclusion:** Every trained person has building access.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** The first rule gets us from trained to badge. The second gets us from badge to access. The shared condition, has a badge, connects them.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Result:** A new rule. It does not establish that anyone is trained.\\r\\n\\r\\nTreat the statements given in an inference question as true. Use them to prove the conclusion. Do not add an unstated fact or connection.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 01 | Facts state what is true\\r\\n\\r\\n**Definition:** A fact tells us something is true about a particular case or group. A fact given in the question is treated as true. A fact you infer must be proved from the given information.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Concrete example:** “Mira is trained.” That tells us something about Mira. It does not tell us whether other people are trained, whether Mira has a badge, or how training relates to badges.\\r\\n\\r\\nA fact can be negative: **“Niko does not have a badge.”** It can describe a group: **“Some staff members are trained.”** That last statement establishes at least one trained staff member, without establishing that every staff member is trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- FACT-SCOPE -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Some reports are missing pages. Does at least one report with missing pages exist? Must every report be missing pages?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** At least one report is missing pages. The statement establishes that such a report exists, but does not establish that every report is missing pages. Other reports could be complete.\\r\\n\\r\\n“Requires approval” and “has received approval” are different facts. A requirement can be established without establishing that it was fulfilled. Keep the complete claim when you read or shorten it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 02 | Rules connect conditions\\r\\n\\r\\n**Definition:** A conditional rule tells us what must be true if a condition is met. It applies to every case that meets that condition. A rule connects conditions; it does not identify a particular case that meets them.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Concrete example:** “Everyone who is trained has a badge.” This connects being trained with having a badge. It says that every trained person actually has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- RULE-PARTS -->\\r\\n\\r\\nLet **T** mean “is trained” and **B** mean “has a badge.” We can write the rule as **T → B**. The arrow points from the starting condition to the required result. If T is true of someone, B must be true of that same person.\\r\\n\\r\\nBeing trained is **sufficient** to establish that someone has a badge: it is enough to trigger this rule. Having a badge is **necessary** for being trained under this rule: a trained person cannot lack a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\nThat does not mean training is the only way to get a badge. It also does not establish that anyone is trained. The rule could hold even if no one met its starting condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Rules can connect different kinds of claims\\r\\n\\r\\nA rule can tell us what someone **should** do or what they **may** do. That is different from telling us what they actually did.\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose **every damaged item should be inspected**. If this item is damaged, it should be inspected. We cannot conclude it has been inspected. But “has not been inspected” is not the same as “it is not true that it should be inspected.” An inspection could be overdue.\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose **a visitor may enter the archive only if the visitor has a badge**. A badge is necessary for permission to enter. The rule is **May enter → Has a badge**. Without a badge, the visitor does not have permission to enter under this rule. Having a badge alone does not establish permission; another requirement might still apply.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- MODALITY -->\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule concerns permission. It does not prove that someone physically cannot enter without permission. Keep the same kind of claim throughout your reasoning: a requirement stays a requirement, advice stays advice, and permission stays permission.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every damaged item should be inspected. This damaged item has not been inspected. Does that violate the rule? What can you conclude about what should happen?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** The item should be inspected. Its not having been inspected does not contradict the claim about what should happen. The rule does not state that every required inspection actually occurs.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 03 | Apply a rule to the same case\\r\\n\\r\\n**Definition:** Applying a rule means finding an established fact that satisfies the rule\'s starting condition, then concluding the required result for **the same case**.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Apply the rule forward\\r\\n\\r\\n**Given fact:** Mira is trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** Everyone who is trained has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** “Mira is trained” satisfies the rule\'s condition, “is trained.”\\r\\n\\r\\n**Therefore:** Mira has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** The rule covers every trained person, including Mira. We have proved a new fact about Mira. We have not made a claim about everyone who has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- APPLY-SEQUENCE -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every file marked confidential requires restricted storage. This file is marked confidential. What follows? Does the rule alone establish that a confidential file exists?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** This file requires restricted storage. The fact establishes that this particular file is confidential. The rule alone establishes a requirement for any confidential file, without establishing that any such file exists.\\r\\n\\r\\n### When the fact denies the result\\r\\n\\r\\nA different fact calls for a different move. Suppose **Niko does not have a badge**, and the same rule says **everyone who is trained has a badge**.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf Niko were trained, the rule would require Niko to have a badge. That would contradict the given fact. So **Niko is not trained**.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Given fact:** Niko does not have a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** Trained → Has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** The fact denies the required result. Restate the rule as **No badge → Not trained**.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Therefore:** Niko is not trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** Training cannot be present while the rule\'s required result is absent. This is the **contrapositive**: reverse the arrow and negate both complete conditions.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe contrapositive states the same rule. It does not deny the original rule, and it is not the unsupported reversal **Has a badge → Trained**.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep qualifiers inside the conditions. For **Damaged → Should be inspected**, the contrapositive starts with “it is not true that it should be inspected” and concludes “not damaged.” Whether the item has actually been inspected is a different claim.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- TWO-MOVES -->\\r\\n\\r\\n### Four facts, one unchanged rule\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule is **Trained → Has a badge**. The rule stays the same. Change only what we know about the person.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fact 1: The person is trained\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** The original starting condition is met.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** The person has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** Every trained person has a badge under this rule. Apply it to this person.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fact 2: The person has no badge\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** The required result is absent. This meets the contrapositive\'s starting condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** The person is not trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** A trained person without a badge would contradict the rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fact 3: The person has a badge\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** We have only the original result. The rule does not say that everyone with a badge is trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** Whether this person is trained remains unknown.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** An untrained visitor with a badge fits the rule. A trained person with a badge also fits it.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fact 4: The person is not trained\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** The original starting condition is absent. The rule only guarantees badges for trained people.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** Whether this person has a badge remains unknown.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** An untrained person could have a badge or lack one. Both situations fit the rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Trained, but no badge** contradicts the rule. The other three combinations fit.\\r\\n\\r\\nNow explore those same moves with a different rule: **Every late application requires special approval.** First identify the supplied fact. Then see whether it triggers the original rule, triggers its contrapositive, or leaves the other condition open.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- FACT-LAB -->\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- CASE-GRID -->\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- POP-QUIZ-1 -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every confidential file requires restricted storage. This file does not require restricted storage. What follows? What if all you know is that it does require restricted storage?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** If it does not require restricted storage, it is not confidential. That is the contrapositive. If it does require restricted storage, its confidentiality remains unknown. A nonconfidential file could require restricted storage for another reason.\\r\\n\\r\\nYou do not have to write every contrapositive automatically. If the fact clearly meets the original starting condition, apply that rule. If the fact denies its result, or a connection is difficult to see, a contrapositive can make the supported move clear.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 04 | Negate the whole requirement\\r\\n\\r\\nSome conditions contain more than one part. Suppose **a document may be released only if it has been reviewed AND authorized**. Permission requires both parts. If either part is missing, the combined requirement is not satisfied.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** May be released → Reviewed AND authorized.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Contrapositive:** Not reviewed OR not authorized → May not be released.\\r\\n\\r\\nTo negate “reviewed AND authorized,” you only need at least one failure. You do not need both parts to fail. “Not both” does not mean “neither.”\\r\\n\\r\\nNow suppose **a visitor may enter only if the visitor has a badge OR an invitation**. This requirement allows either route, including both. To fail the entire requirement, the visitor must have neither.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** May enter → Has badge OR has invitation.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Contrapositive:** No badge AND no invitation → May not enter.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- NEGATION -->\\r\\n\\r\\nNegating a compound condition changes **AND to OR**, or **OR to AND**, while negating each part. For the original AND requirement, one missing part is enough. For the original OR requirement, both routes must be missing.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** A document may be released only if it has been reviewed and authorized. It has been reviewed but not authorized. What follows? Does the contrapositive require us to conclude it was not reviewed?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** The document may not be released. Missing authorization is enough to fail the combined requirement. We do not conclude that review is missing too; the fact says review occurred.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 05 | Link rules to produce a new rule\\r\\n\\r\\n**Definition:** Linking rules means matching the result of one rule to the starting condition of another, so that together they establish a **new conditional rule**.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Link the rules without adding a case\\r\\n\\r\\n**First rule:** Every late application requires special approval.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Second rule:** Every application requiring special approval needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** “Requires special approval” is the result of the first rule and the starting condition of the second. The whole idea matches.\\r\\n\\r\\n**New rule:** Submitted late → Requires special approval → Needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Therefore:** Every late application needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** Consider any application that is late. The first rule requires approval; that satisfies the second rule\'s condition, which requires a signature. This proves the general connection **Late → Needs signature**. It does not establish that any application was late.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Add a fact and use the whole chain\\r\\n\\r\\n**Given fact:** Application J was submitted late.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rules:** Submitted late → Requires special approval → Needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Therefore:** Application J requires special approval and needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rules-only conclusion was a new **if-then rule**. Adding J\'s fact lets us apply it and conclude something about **J**. Do not silently add a case when the information gives you only rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- TWO-OUTPUTS -->\\r\\n\\r\\nYou can also use the chain\'s contrapositive: **Does not need signature → Does not require approval → Was not late**. A fact denying the final result can be carried backward through both connections.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- CHAIN-LAB -->\\r\\n\\r\\n### The whole middle idea must match\\r\\n\\r\\n“Requires special approval” and “has received special approval” are not the same condition. A requirement can remain unfulfilled. A rule ending in the first phrase cannot be linked to one starting in the second unless another statement connects them. Shared words are not enough; match the complete claim, including qualifiers and negation.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every late application requires special approval. Every application that has received special approval has a supervisor\'s signature. Does a late application necessarily have a supervisor\'s signature? Explain the missing connection.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** No. A late application requires approval, but it may not have received it. The second rule applies to applications that have received approval. Nothing says the late application\'s requirement has been fulfilled.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A contrapositive can uncover the match\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose these two rules are given:\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule 1:** Trained → Has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule 2:** Not trained → Requires supervision.\\r\\n\\r\\n“Trained” and “not trained” are opposite ideas in the same position: both are starting conditions. They do not look like a chain yet. Take the contrapositive of the first rule:\\r\\n\\r\\n**Restated rule 1:** No badge → Not trained.\\r\\n\\r\\nNow “not trained” is the result of that restatement and the starting condition of rule 2. We can link them:\\r\\n\\r\\n**New chain:** No badge → Not trained → Requires supervision.\\r\\n\\r\\n**New conditional:** Anyone without a badge requires supervision.\\r\\n\\r\\nAgain, that is a general rule. It does not prove that someone lacks a badge. If a separate fact says Niko lacks a badge, then we can apply the chain and conclude that Niko requires supervision.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- HIDDEN-CHAIN -->\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the matching condition is not visible, try a contrapositive. Here, rewriting **Trained → Has a badge** as **No badge → Not trained** creates the connection. If the match is already visible, link the rules directly.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 06 | Shared beginnings do not create a chain\\r\\n\\r\\nUse only the rules given in this new example. Rules from earlier examples do not carry over.\\r\\n\\r\\nCompare a different pair of rules: **Every late application requires special approval. Every late application needs a supervisor\'s signature.** Both rules start at “late.” Neither starts where the other ends.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- SHARED-TRIGGER -->\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- POP-QUIZ-2 -->\\r\\n\\r\\nIf a fact tells us an application was late, we can apply **both** rules to it. That application requires approval and needs a signature. But we cannot conclude that every application requiring approval needs a signature. An on-time application might require approval for another reason without needing a signature.\\r\\n\\r\\nLikewise, two rules that end at the same idea do not connect their starting conditions. If confidential files require restricted storage and medical files require restricted storage, a confidential file need not be a medical file. Restricted storage could cover both types separately.\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore joining two arrows, find the destination of the first and the starting condition of the second. They must match, including any negation or qualifier. If they do not match, keep the rules separate or see whether a valid contrapositive produces a match.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every member of a review panel has training. Every member of that panel has a security badge. Must everyone with training have a security badge? What follows if Dana is a panel member?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** Training alone does not guarantee a badge. A trained person could be outside the panel. Because Dana is a panel member, both rules apply to Dana: Dana has training and a security badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 07 | Choose the move and test the claim\\r\\n\\r\\nAsk what you have: a fact that can activate a rule, or two rules that can connect? The matching condition determines the move.\\r\\n\\r\\n| What you have | The move | What to check |\\r\\n| --- | --- | --- |\\r\\n| A fact meeting a rule\'s starting condition | Apply the rule forward | Does the whole fact match the whole condition? |\\r\\n| A fact denying a rule\'s required result | Use the contrapositive | Did you reverse and negate both complete ideas? |\\r\\n| Two rules with a matching middle condition | Link the rules | Does the first rule end with the exact condition the second starts with? |\\r\\n| Only the required result, or only the absence of the starting condition | Leave the other side open | Could another explanation fit the rule? |\\r\\n\\r\\nTo disprove a proposed inference, keep every given statement true and make the proposed conclusion false. For example, an on-time application that requires approval fits **Late → Requires approval**, but disproves **Requires approval → Late**.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep the scope of the fact too. **Some applications were submitted late. Every late application requires special approval. Therefore, some applications require special approval.** We cannot change “some” to “all.” The rule covers every late case, but the fact establishes only at least one. Other applications could be on time and require no special approval.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 08 | Mix the moves\\r\\n\\r\\nUse only the information given in each problem. For each problem, write what follows and name the move. If a proposed conclusion is not guaranteed, explain a situation that would make it false while keeping the given statements true.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 1:** Every confidential file requires restricted storage. File K does not require restricted storage. What follows?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** File K is not confidential. Use the contrapositive: does not require restricted storage → not confidential.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 2:** Every late application requires special approval. Every application requiring special approval needs a supervisor\'s signature. Application J was late. What follows?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** Application J requires special approval and needs a supervisor\'s signature. Apply the first rule to the fact, then carry its result through the second rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 3:** Every panel member has training. Every panel member has a security badge. Lee has training. Must Lee have a badge?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** No. Lee could be a trained person outside the panel who has no badge. The two rules share a starting condition. Training alone does not establish that Lee meets it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 4:** A document may be released only if it has been reviewed and authorized. Document M has not been reviewed. What follows? Must it also lack authorization?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** Document M may not be released. One missing requirement is enough. Whether it has authorization remains open; it could have authorization but lack review.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 09 | Take this into your assigned work\\r\\n\\r\\nDo the **conditional-diagramming drills first**, then move to the **LSAT questions**. In the drills, write the complete conditions before shortening them. Check your arrow and its contrapositive. When two rules appear, look for an exact match before combining them.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn the LSAT questions, identify the rule or chain that proves each answer. Check that the answer preserves the complete conditions. **Requires approval** does not become **has received approval**. If you cannot prove an answer, try a counterexample: can the given statements be true while the answer is false?\\r\\n"}'}]
